2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
• Recent revisions to US PCE inflation, UK productivity
and Canadian population estimates are reminders that
statistical measurement is imperfect. Revisions are both a
fact of life and, when they improve measurement, desirable.
• Australian data will also be revised over time, though

not necessarily in the same ways as these latest revisions
abroad. The ABS tends to favour quality over speed,
which reduces the need for early revisions as more data
become available. But revisions can still matter. Population
estimates implied in the initial releases of labour market and
GDP data are especially likely to be revised in the near term.
• Estimates of current trends can change as future data are
published. Shifting seasonal patterns are another source

of revision, especially for monthly CPI components with
short histories. Series that are derived from multiple other
imperfectly measured series are more prone to revision
than quantities that can be measured directly. All these
considerations point to a need for humility when using
recent data to make strong policy pronouncements.
On September 30, the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation,
PCE, was revised down. New methods and data sources were
applied to three components of services inflation (financial

advice, software and legal services), large enough to result in
downward revisions to the overall read on core PCE inflation.
These changes had been pre-announced in June and were
therefore not a surprise. The CPI measure of inflation was not
affected, nor should the revisions materially change your view
of US inflationary pressures.
Some revisions are more consequential for interpretation and
policy. A couple of weeks before the US PCE revision, it was

revealed that the UK Office for National Statistics was revising
down its estimates of average hours worked per worker. The
result was that measured productivity growth was nowhere
near as weak as had been widely believed. As my friend and
counterpart, TS Lombard’s Chief Economist Freya Beamish
commented on social media, “Oh well. Not like any major policy
decisions were taken on the basis of those numbers.” Quite.
Also in September, Statistics Canada revised up its estimates

of Canada’s population, taking the recent growth rates
from outright contraction to still-slow growth a little below
½%yr. The number of temporary residents in Canada is still
falling, but the revised data do a better job of tracking them,
especially those seeking an extension on their permit. (An alert
When reality gets revised
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
for customers who have seen the cross-country population
graph I often use at client events: the Canadian line now looks

different but still shows a sharp slowing.)
There is no fault to be attributed here. Revisions to key
statistics are a part of economic life. Sometimes the underlying
data arrive with a lag, perhaps because of reporting deadlines.
Sometimes the compiling agency improves its methods or
gains access to a better data source. The US and UK examples
above fall into this category. In the Canadian case, better
data and a more accurate definition of scope were involved.

All these revisions should be welcomed. They provide a more
accurate picture of the underlying reality.
Australia, revised
Revisions are a fact of life for Australian statistics, too. I am
still a little scarred by that unexpected –0.5%qtr GDP print
when the September quarter 2016 data were first released on
my first day as RBA Assistant Governor; the figure has since
been revised to 0%qtr and was followed by a big bounce-back
in the December quarter.

The recent revisions mentioned above are less likely to be
replicated in Australia, though. Australia’s CPI is designed such
that the headline (not seasonally adjusted) measure is never
revised. This is because it is used in many legal contracts.
If an error is detected, the ABS makes the adjustment in
subsequent months and quarters rather than revising the
history. Our labour force survey has a much higher response
rate than its UK equivalent – Australians are a compliant lot

when the ABS comes knocking – and measures actual hours
worked more reliably. And our population statistics already
handle the array of temporary resident visas with the Bureau
instead applying a standard 12-month-in-16-month residency
test to count as a resident for the population statistics rather
than using a visa-classification basis.
This last case illustrates Australia’s broader tendency to
favour quality over speed in official statistics. Our CPI and

national accounts data tend to be published a bit later than
peer countries’ data for the same reference period, and our
“Economic data are often revised. Sometimes
the revisions change the story; sometimes
they do not."

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
estimated resident population data is quite a bit lagged
relative to Canada’s (the 12-month-in-16 criteria taking over
a year to confirm). The trade-off is that observers of the
Australian economy must wait longer but can be less worried
about “flash” and “advance” releases of data that can be

quickly revised to tell a completely different story.
Revisions to data still can matter in Australia, and it helps
to be aware of the specific cases where it might occur. For
example, precisely because the population data are very
lagged, the population estimates underlying the initial
releases of the labour force survey and national accounts are
based on preliminary data and get revised later as a matter
of routine. This is why ratios such as the unemployment,

employment-to-population and participation rates provide a
better real-time gauge of labour market conditions than the
monthly change in employment. Similarly, GDP per capita can
be revised even when GDP is not. This type of revision is also
a more likely source of short-term changes in measured hours
worked in the Australian productivity data than the conceptual
change that shifted the UK data.
The usual reporting lags – and occasional correction of

reporting errors by the reporting entity – will always be a factor
in Australia just as it is in other countries. The annual national
accounts, due on 23 October, incorporate additional data, revise
the quarterly estimates and provide first estimates of important
quantities such as the capital stock. Just as importantly, though,
the annual accounts reconcile the different ways of measuring
GDP. In doing so, what we thought we knew about the recent

history of output growth can change.
Every purchase is also a sale, so conceptually, you should get the
same answer for final output regardless of whether you count
how much is produced, how much people spent on it, or how
much income people got for producing it. In practice, though,
the “production”, “expenditure” and “income” approaches do
not always line up until the ABS goes through a more thorough
reconciliation process in compiling the annual accounts.

When the future changes the past
Future data can also change our view of the past. Two good
examples are trend estimation and seasonal adjustment. Future
observations help reveal whether an apparent trend is stable.
As a result, real-time trend estimates can differ considerably
from those calculated with the benefit of later data.
Identifying stable seasonal patterns also requires a reasonable
run of data. Future observations affect whether an earlier

movement is judged to be normal seasonal variation. This
problem is more pronounced for newer series, where there is
not yet enough history to establish what “normal” looks like.
Consider, then, the monthly CPI. This relatively new measure has
only a short run of data with many individual components having
too little history to use standard seasonal adjustment methods.
As Westpac’s senior economist Justin Smirk has previously
highlighted, some components such as footwear are being

‘seasonally adjusted’ by applying Henderson trends, similar
to those used in the ‘trend’ measures of labour market and
national accounts data. For some other components, including
books, furniture, audio-visual and computing equipment and
children’s clothes, certain price falls – usually associated with
sales periods – are just arbitrarily removed from the seasonally
adjusted series. The index is held at its previous month’s level
but otherwise matches the non-seasonally adjusted series.

The seasonally adjusted measures of these CPI components will
inevitably be revised over time, and thus so could the monthly
trimmed mean estimates. This helps explain why the RBA has
said it would, for now, focus on the quarterly trimmed mean
based on the old seasonal factors. That measure will at least
not be buffeted by shifting measurement of seasonality in a
short history. If seasonal patterns are themselves also shifting,
the current underlying trend might look a bit different in a few

years, not just a smoother month-to-month profile.
The broader point here is to remember that economic statistics
are a lens on reality. Sometimes the lens is smudged, sometimes
the scope is off and sometimes what is being read as underlying
trends is wrong. Measures constructed from several imperfect
series – productivity from output and hours worked, or unit labour
costs from productivity and labour costs, or savings rates derived
from income and spending – are especially prone to revision

compared with concepts that can be measured more directly, like
a price. That is a strong argument for humility before making firm
policy pronouncements based on simple data comparisons.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: mulling the minutes
consistent with a labour market that is gradually normalising
than one entering a meaningful downturn.
The September FOMC minutes reinforced a distinctly hawkish
message, with participants noting they had "not seen

sufficient progress on lowering inflation in recent months" and
expressing concern that inflation risks remained "skewed to the
upside". Policymakers highlighted that both core services and
core goods inflation remained elevated, with some attributing
persistent goods price strength to the effects of the AI buildout
despite the sector's traditional disinflationary role. There was
also concern that, after more than five years of inflation above
target, elevated inflation could begin to influence inflation

expectations and wage- and price-setting behaviour. In
contrast, labour market conditions were generally viewed as
stable and "close to maximum employment". While a majority
noted the labour market had strengthened somewhat recently,
several participants highlighted unusually low dynamism,
characterised by low hiring, low layoffs, a low job-finding
rate and elevated long-term unemployment. Importantly,
participants generally judged that aggregate wage growth

was moderate and "consistent with inflation moving toward 2
percent", with several explicitly noting that "the labor market
was not currently a source of inflationary pressures".
While participants broadly expected AI-related investment to
boost productivity growth and potential output over time, several
warned that "the AI buildout could cause aggregate demand
to outpace aggregate supply over the medium term, putting
upward pressure on inflation". Consistent with this view, some

participants had raised their estimate of the neutral policy rate
and several judged the current policy stance to be "not restrictive
or only mildly restrictive". Against this backdrop, all participants
supported the September rate increase, and most assessed
that another increase in the federal funds rate would likely be
appropriate by year-end, although future decisions would remain
data dependent.
The September ISM Services PMI released this week painted

a similar picture of an economy that remains resilient but is
gradually losing momentum. The headline index eased to 54.9
from 55.4 in August, with the decline driven by a sharp fall in
business activity and a 9.4pt drop in new export orders, which
slipped into contractionary territory for the first time in eight
The only data release of note in Australia this week was
the October Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment Survey ,
which reported a 4.7% drop in the headline index. At

80.4, sentiment has tumbled back to deeply pessimistic
levels, similar to the post-pandemic cost-of-living shock
when readings were among the weakest in the survey’s
more than fifty-year history. Indeed, inflation and interest
rate pressures are once again clearly front of mind for
consumers, with both the view on ‘family finances vs a
year ago’ and ‘family finance next 12 months’ falling 8.0%
and 6.4% in the month, respectively. Moreover, the RBA’s

September rate hike looks to have had a significant impact –
sentiment tanked 20% between those surveyed before the
RBA decision and those surveyed afterwards.
Views on the economic outlook were relatively more resilient,
but they still weakened, with the one-year and five-year ahead
sub-indexes falling 1.6% and 0.4% respectively. Consumers
are becoming more ‘on edge’ about job security, albeit not
outright alarmed. The unemployment rate remains relatively

low versus history, although it has drifted up to a cycle high
of 4.6% as labour supply continues to outstrip demand. We
expect it to hold there in next week’s September Labour Force
Survey – see our preview for more detail. All told, with higher
interest rates, renewed inflation pressures and a softening
jobs market all weighing at once, households are entering the
final months of the year on the back foot.
Offshore, the focus was on US data.
Before turning to this week's releases, it is worth noting that

September non-farm payrolls came in softer than expected
across most metrics. Employment rose by only 29k (89k
expected), and there were sizeable downward revisions
to prior months, with a combined 60k removed from July
and August payrolls, leaving gains of just 31k and 29k
respectively. The details pointed to weaker hiring in leisure
& hospitality and education & health services industries,
although construction and manufacturing employment
continued to benefit from ongoing data-centre-related

investment. The unemployment rate edged up to 4.2%, with
softer employment occurring alongside a rise in labour force
participation to 61.8%. Meanwhile wage growth moderated
to 0.1%mth (3.0%yr). Taken together, the report suggests
labour demand is cooling, with the three-month average
pace of job creation slowing to around 50k. However, softer
payroll gains are not necessarily indicative of labour market
deterioration. Lower immigration and ageing demographics

have reduced the pace of employment growth needed to keep
the unemployment rate stable. As such, the report is more

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Fuel, housing and the bumpy road ahead
Higher fuel costs, a weaker exchange rate, and a softer
housing market have prompted a refresh of our near-term
forecasts. Inflation looks set to finish 2026 above 4%, while
growth could lose a little momentum into year-end. Even so,

we continue to expect the RBNZ to pause in October before
lifting the OCR to 3% in December.
New Zealand’s recovery is still moving forward, but the road
has become rougher. Since our last forecast update in August,
global oil and refined-fuel prices have climbed sharply, the
New Zealand dollar has fallen, and longer-term interest rates
have moved higher. At home, the housing market has remained
weaker than we anticipated. None of those developments,

on their own, are enough to derail the recovery. Together,
however, they point to a tougher finish to 2026 for households
and businesses. We have therefore revisited our near-term
inflation, housing and growth forecasts. The broad story
remains one of continued economic expansion and gradually
improving labour demand, but with a more pronounced
inflation spike and slightly less momentum in consumer
spending. Our OCR call is unchanged: a pause at the October

review, followed by a 25 basis point increase to 3% at the
December Monetary Policy Statement.
A temporary inflation reprieve
We continue to think that consumer prices rose 0.7% in the
September quarter. That would lower annual inflation from
4.1% in June to 3.7% in September, broadly consistent with
the monthly price updates published so far. The final monthly
release for the quarter is due on 16 October and will help firm up
that estimate. But any easing in the annual rate is likely to prove

short-lived. We now expect prices to rise 1.1% in the December
quarter, lifting annual inflation to a three-year high of 4.2% by
year-end. That is half a percentage point above our previous
projection and above the RBNZ’s September forecast of 3.9%.
The reason is easy to see at the petrol pump. Higher global
fuel prices have been amplified by a more than 4% fall in
the New Zealand dollar since the start of September. Local
petrol prices have risen by around 50 cents a litre over the

past month, with the nationwide average price of 91 unleaded
reaching a record $3.55 a litre this week. RBNZ Governor
Breman acknowledged a couple of weeks ago that higher
oil prices have tilted the risks around the near-term inflation
outlook to the upside. Importantly, however, the RBNZ remains
focused on the medium-term inflation outlook rather than
trying to offset every movement in volatile fuel prices.
For now, we have left our quarterly inflation forecasts for 2027

unchanged. Even so, the higher starting point at the end of this
year means annual inflation is likely to remain firm through much
of 2027. Our updated profile has inflation at 3.7% in March
2027, 2.6% in June, 2.5% in September and 1.7% in December.
That is broadly similar to the RBNZ’s path through the first three
quarters, although our forecast falls more quickly late in the
year. We will take a fuller look at the 2027 inflation and growth
outlook next week alongside the monthly Market Outlook.

Housing fails to find its feet
The housing market has also delivered a disappointment. We
had expected prices to stabilise through the second half of
2026, but sales volumes have continued to weaken, and the
median time required to sell a property has lengthened to 48
days. Average prices have kept edging lower, despite firmer
conditions in parts of the South Island with greater exposure
to the strong export economy. The latest indicators do not
suggest that a decisive turn is close. We have therefore revised

our forecast for national house price growth in 2026 from a
0.6% rise to a 1.4% decline. That incorporates quarterly falls
of 0.5% in September and 0.8% in December.
The headwinds are familiar: the labour market remains soft,
mortgage rates are higher and employment growth is still
moderate. Those forces are likely to keep the market subdued
for some time, with risks tilted towards another weak year
in 2027. Changes to the taxation of capital gains on investor

housing following the 2026 General Election may add another
potential drag.
Kelly Eckhold
Chief Economist NZ

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Regional outcomes will continue to differ. Tourism- and
agriculture-focused areas should benefit from stronger income
and employment growth, supported by robust commodity export
prices. We have lifted our 2026/27 Fonterra Milk Price forecast to
$9.75/kg and set an opening forecast of $9.50/kg for 2027/28. In

contrast, homeowners in the major North Island cities may have to
wait longer for the wealth boost that comes with rising property
values. Data released this week shows that household wealth
continues to be dragged down by declining housing values.
A little less momentum
The weaker housing backdrop matters for the wider economy.
When petrol absorbs more of the household budget and home
values are drifting lower, consumers tend to become more
cautious. That combination is likely to crimp discretionary

spending in the run-up to Christmas and could take some
steam out of the construction recovery. That’s even though our
own card spending data for September  showed resilience in
consumer spending. Recent business and consumer confidence
surveys have nevertheless remained resilient despite the rise
in energy prices and long-term interest rates. Our short-term
growth forecasts therefore remain broadly intact, but we have
trimmed our December quarter consumer spending projection

and now expect GDP growth of 2.5% over 2026. That is still
a solid result, although the balance of risks has shifted to
the downside.
There are several moving parts to watch. Sustained high fuel
prices would squeeze household purchasing power more than
we have allowed for. The sharp lift in long-term interest rates
may also weigh on activity through 2027, even if shorter-
term borrowing costs evolve as expected. The election result
introduces two-sided risks that are difficult to quantify before

the shape and timing of policy changes are known. Against that,
strong agricultural incomes and signs of improving employment
growth should provide a buffer. We have not changed our 2026
labour market forecasts. Hiring appears to be strengthening
gradually, but it will take time before that improvement
translates into a noticeable fall in the unemployment rate.
The RBNZ should still pause before Christmas
Despite the higher near-term inflation track, we are not

changing our OCR forecast. Since the July Monetary Policy
Review, our view has been that the RBNZ would slow the pace
of increases as the OCR approached the Bank’s estimate of a
neutral 3%. The September meeting clearly signalled a pause
in October and one further rise in December. We continue to
regard that as the most likely path. The latest developments
raise headline inflation but, so far, there is little evidence that
inflation expectations are moving higher or that the fuel shock

is generating broader, persistent price pressure. At the same
time, growth risks warrant some caution.
A pause in October would give the Monetary Policy Committee
time to assess the cumulative effect of the increases already
delivered and to refine its strategy for 2027. Financial
conditions have also tightened from an unexpected source:
longer-term interest rates have risen markedly over the past
six weeks. That increase has at least offset the easing impulse

from the lower exchange rate, even though the weaker
currency is adding to imported inflation in the near term. We
still think the RBNZ is likely to have more work to do in 2027,
but there is plenty of data to come before that judgement
needs to be made.
For now, the best description of the outlook is resilient but
less comfortable. Export incomes remain strong, activity is
expanding and the labour market is beginning to improve. But
households face an awkward combination of record petrol

prices, soft home values and higher borrowing costs. That
mix points to a bumpy end to the year: inflation will be hotter,
spending growth a little cooler and the recovery more uneven
across regions. The RBNZ can afford to wait in October, but if
the economy develops broadly as we expect, a further hike to
3% in December will still be needed.

NZ: Sep REINZ House Sales and Prices (%ann)
Oct 14, Sales Last: –13.0, Prices Last: –0.9
The housing market has remained soft in recent months,
with sales falling to a two-year low and prices edging lower.
Mortgage rates have risen as the RBNZ has initiated a new

tightening cycle, and a growing supply of unsold homes on the
market means that buyers are under no pressure to bid prices
higher. Conditions remain stronger in the south, with prices
up on last year in Canterbury, Otago and Southland, while
Auckland and Wellington are the weakest markets.
NZ: Sep Retail Card Spending (%mth)
Oct 16, Last: –0.9, Westpac f/c: 0.8
We’re forecasting a 0.8% rise in retail spending in September,
reversing the 0.9% fall seen in the previous month. In part, the

forecast rise in spending is due to the sharp increases in petrol
prices in recent weeks. Those higher fuel costs are weighing on
spending in other categories like hospitality. Nevertheless, we
still expect a 0.5% rise in core (ex-fuel) spending. Stats NZ’s
monthly retail spending figures have been very choppy for the
past two years, and we’re likely to see a bounce following last
month’s unexpected falls in categories like furnishings.
NZ: Sep Selected Consumer Prices (%mth)

Oct 16
This will be the last monthly update ahead of the full quarterly
CPI release on 22 October. We’re currently forecasting a 0.7%
quarterly rise in the CPI (+3.7%yr) and will be watching the
detail of the Selected Prices update to pin down that forecast.
In terms of the big categories, we’re forecasting a roughly 6%
rise in petrol costs, along with a 0.7% rise in food prices. That’s
balanced against continued softness in housing rents (which
are the single largest component of the CPI). The key sources

of risk for our forecast are the volatile travel categories, most
of which recorded sizeable drops last month that we expect to
partially reverse in September.

Document 241: First Impressions_ NZIER Quarterly Survey of Business Opinion, September quarter 2026 (2026-10-17)

Report source: Unknown

### Page 1

#businessconditions #newzealand
First Impressions: NZIER Quarterly Survey of Business Opinion,
September quarter 2026
By Michael Gordon
Senior Economist, Westpac NZ
08:35 October 06 2026 Share
Businesses are feeling more optimistic about the months ahead, but current conditions
remain tough for many industries. Cost pressures eased off a little compared to last quarter.
Key results (seasonally adjusted)
General business situation: +40 (Prev: +14)

### Page 2

Trading activity, past three months: -1 (Prev: +1) 
Trading activity, next three months: +15 (Prev: +11) 
Average selling prices, past three months: +27 (Prev: +37) 
Average selling prices, next three months: +45 (Prev: +51)

The NZIER’s quarterly survey of business opinion showed a strong lift in general confidence in
September, despite resurgent fuel prices and the uncertainty around the looming election. Firms were
more confident about their prospects for the next few months, even though their recent performance
remained rough in many industries. 
The survey was held over a relatively compressed period (9 to 28 September), with around 40% of
responses coming back on the first day. Later responses were less confident, as the resurgence in
world oil prices was finally reflected in prices at the pump over the course of the month. 
General business sentiment rose to a net 40% positive compared to a net 14% in June, returning to
around the levels seen in the December quarter last year (before the Middle East conflict). However,
the own-activity measures, which tend to have a closer correspondence with GDP growth, were more
subdued. Trading activity over the last three months was slightly weaker than in June, while
expectations for the quarter ahead saw a more modest four-point increase to +15%. 
Other forward-looking measures were also up for the quarter. Hiring intentions rose strongly to a net
23% positive (although past employment was down). Notably, firms continue to report that workers are
becoming harder to find, despite the ongoing rise in unemployment over the last couple of years. That
said, the proportion of firms citing labour as a factor limiting output declined this quarter. Investment
intentions picked up, more so for plant and machinery than for buildings. 
The results were mixed across industries. Manufacturing and services were more upbeat for the
months ahead but reported substantially weaker performance over the last quarter. In contrast, builders
and merchants reported both stronger past results and greater optimism about the near-term outlook. 
The pricing measures in the survey were down compared to June – not entirely surprising as fuel
prices were lower on average through the September quarter compared to the June quarter. The
notable exception was merchants, who expected both higher costs and prices for the quarter ahead. 
The implications for the RBNZ ahead of its 28 October policy review are unclear – there is certainly no
smoking gun in this report that would demand that the RBNZ abandon the “pause” strategy
communicated at the September MPS. Optimism among businesses remains high despite the obvious
headwinds, but there is still a wide gap between hopes and reality. The pricing measures have eased
compared to last quarter, but that may simply confirm the RBNZ’s expectation that annual inflation will
drop from the 4.1% recorded last quarter as fuel prices eased. The September quarter CPI, released
on 22 October, may be more informative in that respect. 
Michael Gordon, Senior Economist
P: +64 21 749 506

Editorial 
Opportunities from the bond sell-off  
Strategy summary: The global bond sell-off is creating tactical 
and medium-term opportunities. In the US, September’s yield 
surge has improved the risk -reward in 10 -year government 
bonds, presenting an attractive entry point for investors with a 
6-12-month horizon. We believe bond and money markets are 
overly hawkish about the Fed outlook, given disinflation is likely 
to return as impact of oil prices and tariffs ease next year. 
In equities, we stick to quality, especially sectors riding the AI -
led earnings boom. The upcoming Q3 earnings season is likely 
to show AI -investments are powering sectors beyond the 
technology and communications services, such as financials, 
materials and power and electrification. We remain constructive 
on the semiconductor and memory segments. We also expect 
the US Dollar to peak in the next few weeks along with short -
to-medium-term US bond yields. This creates opportunities for 
AUD corporate bonds, Emerging Market USD bonds and gold. 
Attractive risk -reward in US 10 -year government bonds.  
The US 10-year bond yield rose more than 50bps in September 
(biggest monthly rise since 2022) and 115bps this year, amid 
expectations of economic resilience, higher Fed rates, fuel -
driven inflation, AI-related capital demand and fiscal concerns. 
Valuations appear close to fair value, improving the 6-12-month 
risk-reward. Bond arithmetic suggests losses of less than 1% if 
the yield rises to 6%, vs. more than 10% gains if it falls to 4.5%. 
Resilient US economic activity, but softer -than-expected 
inflation. September data showed robust US employment 
(jobless claims, ADP) and core capital-goods orders. However, 
consumer confidence remains depressed, real disposable 
income is flat and households are drawing on savings to sustain 
spending. Against this divided backdrop, core PCE inflation - 
the Fed’s preferred gauge - was softer than expected at 0.2% 
m/m and 3.0% y/y. A revised method to measure PCE inflation 
also indicate that annual core PCE inflation was 35bps lower 
than previously reported, narrowing the gap with CPI inflation. 
Markets appear overly hawkish on the Fed. Money markets 
price about 85bps of tightening over the next 12 months. A 
strong labour market (September’s non -farm payrolls data is 
due tonight) may justify one or two further hikes, but aggressive 
tightening after the sharp rise in bond yields – which has 
already tightened financial conditions - could threaten financial 
stability. US small- and mid-cap equities have already fallen 8–
9% from recent peaks over the past couple of months, 
materially underperforming large caps, as energy and 
borrowing-cost concerns intensify. W hile some Fed speakers 
remain hawkish, we  agree with New York Fed President 
Williams’ that there is no urgency to tighten. We see little 
chance of an October Fed rate hike and expect inflation data 
for Sep-Nov determining whether the Fed moves in December. 
Stay with quality equities and AI beneficiaries.  US large 
caps have absorbed the rise in bond yields relatively well, 
supported by expectations of robust earnings growth led by AI-
related investment. We continue to favour quality companies 
and sectors benefiting from this AI cycle. Semiconductors and 
memory remain preferred exposures: structural AI demand 
should extend the memory upcycle, while long -term supply 
agreements provide better revenue visibility. The opportunity is 
also broadening beyond technology and communication 
services into financials, materials, power and electrification. 
Opportunities in AUD corporate bonds and gold.  The USD 
rebound alongside higher US yields offers a tactical opportunity 
to add higher -yielding Emerging Market USD and non -USD 
bonds. We remain positive on AUD corporate bonds, which 
offer a yield premium over comparable US credit. Our 
constructive medium-term AUD/USD view could add returns for 
USD-based investors over the next 6 –12 months. We would 
also use gold’s recent pullback to add exposure, supported by 
sustained Emerging Market central bank demand. We continue 
to view USD 4,000/oz as important technical support for gold.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya

Top client questions 
  Do you expect more RBA rate hikes after its less hawkish 
guidance? What is your view on the AUD and AUD bonds? 
Our view: We expect the RBA to leave rates unchanged, but 
retain the option to hike again should inflation intensify. We 
maintain our medium-term constructive AUD view and maintain 
our opportunistically bullish AUD corporate bonds idea. 
Rationale: The RBA’s forward guidance remains data dependent, 
explicitly allowing further tightening if needed as inflation remains too 
high.  At 4.60%, Australia’s cash rate is currently 60–85bp above the 
Fed’s 3.75–4.00% range. Thus, even with our two additional 25bp s 
Fed hikes expectation, Australian rates would hold a modest positive 
rate differential. We expect the AUD to strengthen once the Fed 
signals that US rates are near their peak, which should allow resilient 
Australian fundamentals to reassert themselves. Key risks are 
persistently high US yields, weaker China growth or a sharper 
deterioration in global risk sentiment. 
We believe risk-reward has become more attractive for AUD bonds 
following the bear -flattening of the AUD yield curve. Markets are 
already pricing in one more RBA hike through end-2027, presenting 
an attractive opportunity to earn a yield even as increasingly 
restrictive financial conditions reinforc e disinflation over time. 
Meanwhile, the positive interest rate differential with the US supports 
the currency outlook, supporting the case for AUD bonds. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  What does the incoming US data tell us about the path for 
US 10-year bond yields from here? 
Our view: The risk -reward in US 10 -year yields has turned 
attractive following their move higher amid incoming US data. 
Rationale: Recent US data has indeed started to show softer-than-
expected inflation. However, market technicals are likely temporarily 
delaying a move lower in bond yields, with reports suggesting the  
rise in yields is creating forced selling amongst hedge funds and real 
estate investment trusts. Nevertheless, we do not expect this to be 
an extended selloff. We have initiated an opportunistic bullish idea 
on US 10-year government bonds this week and continue to believe 
this idea offers an attractive risk/reward on a 6-12-month horizon as 
yields become attractive enough to entice buyer flow to return. On a 
broader allocation basis, we retain our structural preference for 3-7-
year maturities in bonds. 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

Australia-US interest rate differentials and positive

### Page 5

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  How should investors position in the GBP ahead of the UK 
budget announcement next month? 
Our view: We maintain a medium-term constructive GBP view, 
but keep near-term exposure measured ahead of the 28 October 
Budget. We would view any pre -Budget weakness as an 
opportunity to gradually build GBP exposure. 
Rationale: The government’s decision to adjust the pension triple 
lock from 2030 is an early indication of greater willingness to tackle 
longer-term spending pressures, reinforcing Chancellor Healey’s 
commitment to fiscal discipline and meeting the fiscal rules. Markets 
are likely to scrutinise the Budget closely for evidence that this 
discipline extends to near -term tax and spending decisions. A 
credible Budget should help contain pressure on UK government 
bond yields and support GBP. Any fiscal slippage remains a key risk.  
Near term, we prefer measured GBP exposure as elevated US yields 
and repricing towards further Fed tightening could continue to 
support the USD. Over the medium term, we expect GBP to rebound 
as the Fed eventually signals the peak of its tightening cycle. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  How would different US mid-term election outcome 
scenarios affect the outlook for US equities? 
Our view: We believe election outcomes will have a limited 
impact over the next 12 months. We remain positive on US 
equities driven by fundamental earnings growth.  
Rationale: Over the past 50 years, the ruling US President’s party 
has retained control of both chambers of Congress on only two 
occasions (1978 and 2002). All other elections result ed in an 
opposition sweep or a divided government.  
Based on the latest prediction markets, it appears that an opposition 
sweep (both the House and Senate flips from Republican to 
Democrats) is more likely than a divided government ( in which the 
House flips to Democrats , while the Senate remains under 
Republican control). A scenario where Republicans retain a majority 
is least likely per prediction markets. 
While mid -term elections may introduce near -term volatility, we 
caution against overreaction. Historically, the average return for the 
S&P 500 following an opposition sweep is 17% over the next 12 
months, but the average return following a divided government is not 
significantly worse, at 15%. We prefer to base our investment views 
on fundamental earnings growth, where investor attention should 
focus on the upcoming Q3 earnings season. 
—  Jason Wong, Senior Equity Analyst

### Page 6

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What is the outlook for  the memory chip and broader 
semiconductors industry? 
Our view: We remain positive on semiconductors and memory 
chips. Structural AI demand should prolong the memory 
upcycle while long-term agreements improve revenue visibility. 
Rationale: Recent industry commentary points to tighter memory 
supply-demand conditions through 2028, reinforcing our view that AI 
demand is likely to extend the memory upcycle. Furthermore, adoption 
of long-term agreements improves revenue and earnings visibility. 
Despite debates around “Pacing the Frontier”, we have seen a flurry 
of new model releases, along with the introduction of new consumer 
AI agents capable of comparing prices online and making 
purchases. These are early days, but the path is open for a business-
to-agent (B2A) economy where businesses increasingly interact with 
AI assistants acting on customers’ behalf. As these applications are 
increasingly inference intensive, wider adoption should support 
demand for memory, with its share of global semiconductor 
revenues projected to rise from 30.7% in 2025 to 66.7% in 2027. 
Against this backdrop, we maintain our Opportunistic idea on Global 
Semiconductors. Given memory  chips’ cyclical nature, investors 
should avoid overconcentration in memory names and maintain a 
diversified exposure across Big Tech and semiconductors. 
—  Ryan Goh, Investment Strategist 
  Does a US bond yield stress test raise the risk to your 
constructive view on US equities? 
Our view: We have a target for the S&P 500 index of 8400 over 
the next 12 months. A scenario of a sustained rise in the US 10-
year government bond yield to 5.6% could lead to negative 
returns from the current S&P500 level (7,666). 
Rationale: Our base case 12-month target for the S&P 500 is 8,400, 
which assumes 2027 S&P 500 EPS of USD 420 and a 20x forward 
P/E multiple. A rise in bond yields would have an impact on equity 
valuations, as a higher discount rate reduces the present value of 
future earnings. W e stress -test our target under different 10 -year 
yield and earnings scenarios.  
A rise in bond yields would arguably be associated with a stronger 
growth environment and hence, even higher earnings. However, 
assuming unchanged EPS at our base case of USD 420, a 
(sustained) rise in the 10 -year bond yield to 5.6% would lead to 
negative returns from the current level  if not offset by a further 
improvement in earnings growth . However, in o ur base case  we 
expect the 10-year yield is more likely to fall from here. 
—  Fook Hien Yap, Senior Investment Strategist

Document 317: The end of the safety net_ Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers (2026-09-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The end of the safety net_ Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers (2026-09-28).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

The end of the safety net:  
Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Macro Strategist

28 September 2026 
For several decades, financial markets and the wider economy benefited from an implicit 
safety net. If a slowdown or a downturn arrived, there was a monetary put from central 
banks, and a fiscal put from governments. The monetary put would see interest rates 
slashed and liquidity injected via QE. The fiscal put would see governments run larger 
deficits to cushion any downturn. These policy stabilisers smoothed out the business 
cycle, and supported asset valuations too. 
These policy interventions were increasingly normalised from the late-1980s, and became 
more aggressive with each passing cycle. This reached its peak during the Covid-19 
pandemic, when governments ran fiscal deficits unprecedented in peacetime, whilst 
many central banks expanded their balance sheets with huge asset purchases. 
But after an era of ever-bigger interventions, these old stabilisers now face major 
obstacles. On the fiscal side, policy is constrained today by sovereign yields at multi-year 
highs, alongside the highest debt-to-GDP ratios in several decades. Then on the monetary 
side, policy is constrained by persistent inflation that’s increasingly supply-driven. 
This represents a big shift from the last few decades, but it does echo previous regimes 
where the macroeconomic stabilisers were either blunted or left unused. A good example 
is the 1970s experience, when stagflation and supply shocks posed a dilemma to 
traditional policy tools. Even further back, the pre-WWI environment of the classical gold 
standard and balanced budget orthodoxy saw governments play a very different and more 
limited role in macroeconomic stabilisation. 
Looking forward, this environment means policymakers will have a much more difficult 
time stabilising the business cycle in the coming years. So absent a sharp fall in yields or a 
collapse in inflation, higher macro volatility will be here to stay.

### Page 2

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
1. Background: How the stabilisers used to work.
Economic policymakers have long used interventions to stabilise output. This
became increasingly fashionable after WWII, when Keynesian economics
established a more active use for fiscal policy to stimulate the economy in a
downturn. This meant budget deficits became a more regular feature of life, rather
than used solely for wartime emergencies.

It was from the late 1980s, however, that the extent of policy interventions became
increasingly sizeable. Indeed, it was here that the origins of a central bank “put” for
financial markets began, in the crisis of Black Monday in 1987. That was a huge
stock market crash, where the S&P 500 fell over 20% in a single day, marking the
biggest daily decline in its entire history. The size of the falls even went beyond the
Great Depression, raising fears of wider contagion, particularly if it were to spark a

liquidity crisis that caused financial institutions to fail. However, the Federal
Reserve responded by cutting rates and providing liquidity, offering a
macroeconomic stabiliser against the prospect of wider economic stress. This
offered support against a wider downturn, and the US economic expansion
continued until 1990.
Whilst the genesis of a central bank “put” came in 1987, the idea gained further
traction in the late-1990s, with the Asian financial crisis in 1997, followed by the

Russian financial crisis in 1998. Fears of an economic contraction mounted,
particularly after issues at Long-Term Capital Management (LTCM) raised concerns
that the US wasn’t immune from these global pressures. So once again, the Fed
delivered three 25bp rate cuts pre-emptively, seeking to support the economy.
A few years later, the bursting of the dot com bubble in early 2000 set the stage for
yet further stimulus. As equity markets declined, the Fed began to cut rates from

early 2001, ultimately delivering 475bps of cuts that year that took rates from 6.5%
to just 1.75%. That was followed up by further rate cuts in 2002 and 2003, leaving
rates at 1% by mid-2003 into 2004. Moreover, the Bush tax cuts were also delivered
in 2001 and 2003, acting as a fiscal stimulus as the wider US economy entered
recession.
In hindsight, these moves seem modest compared to what then happened in 2008,
when the Global Financial Crisis led to even bigger policy interventions. Suddenly,

the world faced the biggest economic contraction since the Great Depression, with
genuine fears that a repeat might happen. To avoid this, policymakers launched into
the biggest economic stimulus ever seen in a peacetime environment. That
included massive fiscal stimulus, with the US federal deficit at 9.8% of GDP in 2008,
the highest since 1945. Moreover, central banks slashed rates to the zero lower
bound, and many like the Federal Reserve launched into Quantitative Easing

programmes, purchasing assets to ease financial conditions even more. Over the
coming years, many began pledging to keep rates low well into the future,
pioneering the concept of forward guidance.
These levels of policy intervention weren’t confined to the immediate crisis
however. In the subsequent years, active attempts were made to prolong and
extend the business cycle, particularly as the 2010s became an era of low inflation
and low interest rates. In this environment, borrowing was historically cheap, and

low inflation meant central banks could cut rates without seeing a big overshoot of
their targets. Indeed, both the Fed and the ECB undershot their 2% inflation target
on average in the 2010s, and the ECB took their own deposit rate into negative
territory in 2014.

By 2019, the year before the pandemic, the intellectual climate had continued to
move in the direction of more policy intervention. Government bond yields hit new

lows, with Germany’s 10-year bund yield at -0.7% in summer 2019. This
environment of negative yields meant borrowing was basically a “free lunch”, and
that summer also saw the Fed begin a 75bp easing cycle, as inflation was below
their target and they wanted to sustain the expansion.
The peak of this policy interventionism was seen with the Covid-19 pandemic in
2020. Just as in 2008, the world was facing a huge and sudden economic
contraction. Moreover, the consensus among policymakers was that they could

have done more to speed up the recovery after 2008, potentially avoiding years of
very high unemployment. As such, 2020 saw the most aggressive economic
stimulus packages ever seen in a peacetime environment, well above those in 2008.
Central banks immediately slashed rates back to the zero lower bound, pledged to
keep rates low for years to come, and launched into major QE programmes again.
Then on the fiscal side, record peacetime deficits were run. Indeed, the US federal

deficit of 14.5% of GDP in 2020 was the highest since 1945.

### Page 4

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
During the pandemic period, many initially expected these interventions to remain
for years to come. After all, in mid-2020, financial markets continued to price a
continuation of the low inflation and low interest rates of the 2010s. This belief was
clear on several fronts, and the 10-year Treasury yield closed at just 0.51% in August
2020. Meanwhile, the Federal Reserve even adjusted its own framework to allow for

average inflation targeting, i.e. permitting an overshoot of the inflation target after
periods of an undershoot. This reflected the consensus view that inflation was
unlikely to be a serious threat.
But as we now know, inflation returned in a serious way after the pandemic, forcing
central banks into rate hikes. Meanwhile, public debt-to-GDP ratios rose
significantly thanks to the pandemic stimulus, helping to push up sovereign bond
yields too. In other words, many of the usual policy tools of the previous few

decades were either blunted or no longer available.
2. The Monetary Constraint: High inflation means the central
bank put isn’t available
In the pre-2020 world described above, central banks regularly grappled with how
to generate enough inflation. In part, this was thanks to several structural forces
that put downward pressure on global prices. For instance, deepening
globalisation acted as a positive supply shock that kept costs down. Meanwhile, the

expansion of the global labour force in these years also boosted the supply side. In
other words, inflation was low and central banks generally had the space to cut
interest rates when they needed to.
But since the pandemic, we have entered a very different environment of above-
target inflation across many of the world’s biggest economies. Initially, that was
caused by a huge wave of pent-up demand after the pandemic, alongside
disruption to supply chains and labour markets. But the subsequent years also

brought further shocks. Those included Russia’s invasion of Ukraine in early 2022,
driving a big spike in European energy prices, and pushing Euro Area inflation above
10% briefly. In 2026, the closure of the Strait of Hormuz then acted as a fresh energy
shock, pushing inflation further away from target and contributing to a fresh
tightening cycle from the major central banks.
Figure 4: In the 2020s, inflation has generally been above central bank targets

### Page 5

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Alongside those shocks, the structural forces keeping inflation down have gone
into reverse. For instance, demographic trends have seen working-age populations
shrink in many places. Meanwhile, the trend towards deeper globalisation has
stalled, and the focus has turned to near-shoring and supply-chain resilience, rather
than locating production in the cheapest place. Simultaneously, other trends like

the push for higher defence spending are also adding to demand-side pressures.
Because of all this, the 2020s so far have brought persistently above-target inflation,
and central bankers have found it much more difficult to ease policy in response to
growth wobbles or financial stability fears.
This initially became apparent in 2022, amidst the biggest inflation wave for several
decades. Central banks had to keep hiking rates, even in the face of slowing growth

and financial market stress. Indeed, despite the S&P 500 being in bear market
territory by mid-2022, the Federal Reserve still launched into a series of 75bp hikes,
the biggest hikes since the 1990s. Then in 2023, despite the collapse of Silicon
Valley Bank and fears of wider financial contagion, the Fed and ECB still continued
with their rate hikes to combat inflation.
That tightening cycle in 2022-23 was a significant moment, because high inflation

meant monetary policy could not simply act as a stabiliser against growth or
financial stress. After all, policymakers realised that if they didn’t act to tackle high
inflation, they risked a repeat of the 1970s and the potential for even harsher
monetary tightening to get inflation under control again.
Today in 2026, this is a familiar problem again. We are in a globally synchronised
hiking cycle, with the Fed, ECB and Bank of Japan all having delivered rate hikes.

So far at least, global growth has been robust and financial conditions remain
accommodative by historical standards. But because inflation remains clearly
above target, central banks don’t have the space they did in the pre-pandemic world
to simply cut rates at the first sign of difficulty. There is still an inflation mandate to
be achieved, and given we have now seen multiple years of elevated inflation, there
is a much higher risk of inflation expectations becoming unanchored in that

context, particularly if another shock occurred.
3. The Fiscal Constraint: High yields and high public debt
mean fiscal stimulus isn’t easily available either
Just as monetary policy is constrained by high inflation, fiscal policy today is also
constrained by higher yields and higher public debt. This is a big transition from the
world of 1980-2020, when yields were generally falling throughout. Indeed, despite
all the fiscal stimulus around the GFC, the continuous decline in yields over the

2010s meant that debt dynamics remained manageable, because lower yields
helped to offset the impact of higher debt.
Since the pandemic however, we’ve been in a very different world, as the dual
forces of higher debt and higher yields have made debt dynamics far more
unfavourable around the world. And this isn’t just a nominal story, as real yields
have also risen to multi-year highs as well.

In essence, there are 4 new constraints around fiscal stimulus.
First, is the significant rise in global yields. By definition, the cost of incurring debt
has grown, in both nominal and real terms. Relative to the pre-pandemic baseline

in early 2020, the 10-year US Treasury yield has risen from 1.9% to 5.2%. Similarly
in Germany, the 10-year bund yield has risen from -0.2% to 3.6%. Moreover, with
yields already elevated, any further borrowing risks crowding out the private sector,
and further dampening growth. And for governments, interest spending has
ramped up significantly. For instance, US net interest spending as a share of GDP
exceeded its recent peak from the early 1990s in the most recent fiscal year, and the

CBO forecast this is set to continue.
Figure 6: US Net Interest Costs (% of GDP) with CBO Forecasts

Source : CBO, Deutsche Bank
The second constraint is that public debt itself is now at a higher level. This interacts
with the higher yields mentioned above, because even though yields have only
returned to their levels from the mid-2000s, that is much more impactful because
they are being paid on a much larger debt pile. Using IMF numbers for consistency,

net public debt across the advanced economies has risen from 52% of GDP in 2000,
to 80% in 2025, with further increases projected to 2030. This means the fiscal
space available to governments is more limited than it used to be.

### Page 7

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 7
The third constraint on fiscal stimulus is the high starting level of budget deficits. So
to generate a meaningful fiscal stimulus, it would require deficits to be pushed up
to even higher levels than they are today. But that in turn would add to the risks of
bond market disruption that cascades through the economy, leading to higher
borrowing costs for corporates and consumers as well.
The fourth constraint is higher inflation. When inflation is high, any fiscal stimulus

that boosts demand risks pushing up prices rather than boosting output, and in turn
makes monetary tightening more likely that could offset the impact of the stimulus.
This trade-off was apparent in 2022, when central banks were hiking rates
aggressively, and even further back in the inflationary environment of the 1970s. In
a world of high inflation and supply constraints, fiscal stimulus is more likely to
overheat the economy.
4. Are there any other eras of history where the stabilisers

weren’t available?
Whilst history never quite repeats exactly, there have been other eras in which the
traditional policy stabilisers were either unavailable or severely blunted, which hold
some lessons for our own time.
The pre-WWI classical gold standard
Before WWI, the international monetary system was known as the gold standard,
in which currencies were convertible into a fixed quantity of gold. In practice
however, this acted as a huge constraint on macroeconomic stabilisation. After all,

monetary policy had to maintain the convertibility of gold at a fixed parity. This
meant contemporary options like QE weren’t available in a crisis. Meanwhile on the
fiscal side, governments generally adhered to balanced budget orthodoxy. This was
before Keynes wrote the General Theory, so the modern understanding of
countercyclical deficit spending was not in place. In the US, there wasn’t even a
central bank until the Federal Reserve’s establishment in 1913.

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
Without modern policy stabilisers however, this was also an era of huge
macroeconomic volatility. There was a major financial panic across Europe and the
US in 1873, which marked the start of the so-called “Long Depression”. In the US,
that contraction lasted until 1879, which in length was even longer than the Great
Depression contraction of 1929-33. And this was followed by other major panics,

including the 1893 panic that led to another US contraction, along with the 1907
panic that saw the Dow Jones almost halve relative to its 1906 peak.
The stagflationary 1970s
The more recent example comes from the 1970s, another era of multiple supply
shocks. The biggest were the two oil shocks in 1973 and 1979, which both
represented negative supply shocks that pushed up inflation and hurt growth in the
US and Europe. However, this meant policymakers faced a dilemma. If central

banks cut rates, that would add to the inflationary pressures and risk worsening the
wage-price spirals already underway. Meanwhile, if fiscal stimulus were pursued,
that would also push up inflation.
Figure 8: US CPI (year-on-year % change)

This context meant that policy couldn’t smooth out the business cycle as
successfully as it had in other decades. Indeed, in the decade from the first oil shock

in 1973, the US experienced 3 separate recessions. That is significantly more
turbulent than the last 40 years, when there have been just 4 recessions in total, or
once a decade on average. For financial markets too, the 1970s were a terrible
period for asset performance, with both equities and bonds losing ground in real
terms across multiple countries.
5. Implications
If economic policymakers can’t use traditional monetary and fiscal policy tools to

stabilise output and smooth the business cycle, then this will result in higher macro
volatility.
If we look back at the last 40 years, it is notable that the 5 most recent US expansions
all rank in the top 7 longest expansions since business-cycle dating began. This has
several causes, and some of it reflects a move away from an agriculture-based
economy where output was dependent on factors like the weather. But a key role
has also been played by pre-emptive policy interventions, such as cutting rates to

stop a slowdown turning into a recession. Indeed, recent examples include the a
dovish shifts in monetary policy after the 1987 stock market crash, the 1997-98

### Page 9

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
financial crises, and the mid-cycle slowdown of 2015-16.
Beyond the actual policy interventions, these moves also had a confidence effect
that supported financial markets. The fact that policymakers stood ready to take
action helped create a virtuous circle, as optimism on the economic outlook
boosted financial asset values, supported wealth effects, and avoided a tightening
in financial conditions that would itself cause growth to slow.

Clearly, policy is not as constrained today as in some periods of history. There is no

longer a gold standard where the convertibility needs to be maintained. And unlike
the 1970s, inflation is not in double-digits. Nevertheless, this is still a more
constrained world than we have been used to in the last 4 decades. Higher inflation
and more frequent supply shocks are constraining monetary policy. Meanwhile,
higher yields and higher public debt levels are constraining fiscal policy. We have
already seen the impacts since the pandemic, most notably in 2022, when central

banks hiked rates to combat the inflation surge, and there was a simultaneous
selloff across bonds and equities of a kind unseen in modern times.
Looking forward, this environment will offer new challenges of the sort we haven't
been used to in recent decades. The policy trade-offs are now more acute, and if
pre-emptive stabilisation is no longer available, then higher macro volatility lies
ahead.

Document 366: “大多头”Yardeni下调标普年底目标：盈利不是问题，估值才是

Source PDF: all report pdf/“大多头”Yardeni下调标普年底目标：盈利不是问题，估值才是.pdf

### Page 1

Yardeni Research 将标普 500 年底⽬标价下调⾄ 7900 点，估值⽬标下调⾄ 18.6 倍，主因系
避险情绪压低估值⽽⾮盈利恶化。尽管⾯临避险情绪与政策⻛险， Yardeni 仍看好企业盈利
及中⻓期经济，认为回调中买⼊机会显现。
摘要：
知名市场研究机构 Yardeni Research 下调标普 500 年底⽬标价⾄ 7900 点，理由是估值压缩⻛险上
升，⽽⾮盈利前景恶化。
9 ⽉中旬以来持续发酵的避险情绪正在进⼀步压低股票估值倍数。Yardeni Research 将标普 500
年底⽬标从此前的 8400 点（该⽬标此前已被推迟⾄ 2027 年中）下调⾄ 7900 点，同时将年底远期
市盈率⽬标从 19.8 倍下调⾄ 18.6 倍。
与此同时，市场⾯临多重压⼒叠加：⾼于预期的 9 ⽉ CPI 数据推升美联储年内加息预期， AI 交易
热度明显降温，科技股遭遇⼤幅抛售。标普 500 周三下跌 1.1% ⾄ 7718.94 点，纳斯达克综合指数
与罗素 2000 均下跌 1.9% ，费城半导体指数（ SOXX ）重挫 4.5% 。

估值承压，⽬标价下调
Yardeni Research 明确将当前市场隐患定性为估值问题，⽽⾮盈利问题。该机构维持 2027 年标
普 500 每股盈利预测 425 美元不变，与市场普遍预期的 423.08 美元相差⽆⼏，显⽰其对企业盈利
及经济前景仍保持乐观，并预计 2029 年前不会出现衰退。
然⽽，该机构认为， 9 ⽉中旬以来的避险情绪不会轻易消散，将持续压制估值倍数。基于此判
断，其将年底远期市盈率⽬标由 19.8 倍下调⾄ 18.6 倍，对应年底标普 500 ⽬标价从 8400 点降⾄
7900 点。 " 我们担忧的不是盈利，⽽是股票估值， "Yardeni 写道。
CPI 超预期，加息概率骤升
通胀数据构成本轮市场承压的重要触发因素。 9 ⽉ CPI 环⽐上涨 0.6% ，为今年 3 ⽉以来最⼤单⽉
涨幅，主要受能源价格飙升 9.3% 驱动。核⼼ CPI 环⽐上涨 0.3% ，⽬前已有 52% 的 CPI 分项年化
通胀率超过 3% 。
耐⽤品价格环⽐上涨 0.9% ，其中电脑及电⼦产品价格录得近三个⽉最⾼通胀率， Yardeni 将此归

因于 AI 驱动的半导体需求。
热⻔ CPI 数据迅速重燃市场对美联储加息的担忧， 10 ⽉加息概率从 21% 跳升⾄ 36% ，盘中⼀度触
及 45% ，市场预期年内或有两次加息。尽管如此， Yardeni 指出，10 年期美债收益率⽬前呈现企
稳迹象，部分原因在于通胀保值债券（ TIPS ）仍提供接近 2.90% 的具有吸引⼒的实际收益率。
AI 交易降温，科技股领跌
科技板块成为本轮调整的震中。 Yardeni Research 指出，"AI 交易正在变得混乱，竞争加剧以及
市场对巨额 AI 投资回报的质疑情绪升温。 "费城半导体指数单⽇重挫 4.5% ，集中体现了市场对 AI
相关投资前景的重新审视。
新晋云计算基础设施提供商 CoreWeave 股价暴跌约 16% ，市场对其偿债能⼒的担忧持续升温，
进⼀步拖累板块情绪。

Document 368: 「社外取締役が議長」は何を変えるのか ～プライム市場で118社、大型企業で選任進む～

Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-08
Pages: 4

### Page 1

Flash Insights

2026 年 10 月 8 日

1/4 
「社外取締役が議長」は何を変えるのか 
～プライム市場で 118 社、大型企業で選任進む～ 
 資産運用調査部 シニア研究員  河谷 善夫

1．社外取締役の取締役会議長選任は緩やかに拡大 
筆者は、昨年8 月中旬時点でのプライム市場上場会社のうち、社外取締役を取締役会議長に選
任している会社について分析したレポート（河谷、2025）を公表し、2022 年 7 月上旬時点より、
その数が増加していることを示した。本稿では、2026 年9 月末の時点でのプライム市場における
社外取締役の取締役会議長選任の状況をフォローアップした上で、最後に社外取締役の取締役会
議長選任の意味について考える。

資料 1 社外取締役の取締役会議長選任会社数の推移

（出所）東証公表資料を基に第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

資料 1 のとおり、2025 年 8 月から2026 年 9 月にかけて会社数は9 社増加し、プライム市場での
比率は 6.7％から7.6％と0.9％ポイント上昇した。2022 年以降、年平均で概ね1％ポイントの上
昇ペースが続いている。このグラフだけでは見えないが、2025 年8 月から2026 年9 月にかけて継
続してプライム市場に上場している会社では17 社が社外取締役を新たに取締役会議長に選任して
おり（注1）、新規選任の動きは続いている。 
また、118 名中 117 名が独立社外取締役であり、社外取締役議長はほぼすべて独立社外取締役で
ある（注2）。

### Page 2

2/4 
2．特定の機関設計に限らず普及、ただし社外取締役議長はなお少数 
資料 2 は、会社の機関設計別の2022 年、2025 年、2026 年のプライム市場での社外取締役の取
締役会議長選任会社数と割合を示したものである。

資料 2 機関設計別の社外取締役の取締役会議長選任状況（プライム市場）

（出所）資料 1 と同じ

2026 年では、社外取締役を取締役会議長に選任している会社は、監査役設置会社で5.3％、監
査等委員会設置会社で6.0％、指名委員会等設置会社で42.7％である。指名委員会等設置会社の
割合は依然として高いが、2022 年からの増加状況をみると、社外取締役を取締役会議長とする動
きは、指名委員会等設置会社以外にも徐々に広まっており、昨年のレポートで確認した傾向は続
いているといえる。 
なお、これも資料で示していないが、取締役の過半を独立社外取締役とする会社でも、社外取
締役を取締役会議長とする会社は約2 割にとどまり、2025 年 8 月から2026 年 9 月にかけて大きな
変化はなかった（注3）。

3．社長を議長とする会社の減少と、大型企業で先行する社外取締役議長選任 
資料 3 では、2022 年、2025 年、2026 年のプライム市場での取締役会議長の属性の変化をグラフ
として示した。

資料 3 取締役会議長の属性の変化（プライム市場）

（注）「その他の取締役等」には、会長・社長以外の執行役を兼任する取締役を含む。 
（出所）資料 1 と同じ

### Page 3

3/4 
2022 年からの変化をみると、社長を取締役会議長とする会社の割合は71.4％から65.6％へ
5.8％ポイント低下する一方、社外取締役を議長とする会社の割合は、3.6％から 7.6％へ4.0％ポ
イント上昇し、会長を議長とする会社の割合も1.5％ポイント上昇している。このような社長を取
締役会議長とする会社の割合の低下、社外取締役を議長とする会社の割合の上昇は、取締役会議
長と社長を分離するという、より大きな流れの一部とみることができるだろう。 
次に、どのような会社で社外取締役の取締役会議長選任が進んでいるのかをみるため、2026 年
9 月末時点のプライム市場上場会社について、時価総額ランク別の選任割合を示した（資料4）。

資料 4 時価総額ランク別社外取締役議長選任割合（プライム市場・2026 年 9 月末）

（出所）Bloomberg、東証公表資料データを基に第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

資料 4 からは、時価総額の大きい企業ほど、社外取締役の取締役会議長選任が進んでいるとい
える。なお、社外取締役を取締役会議長に選任している会社の社数の割合は7.6％だが、時価総額
では 19％を占める。また、社外取締役が議長となっている会社の39％が時価総額１兆円以上の会
社である。時価総額が大きい企業ほど選任率が高く、大型企業での社外取締役の取締役会議長選
任が相対的に進んでいる。

4．最後に 
ここまで、社外取締役の取締役会議長選任状況について確認してきた。このような社外取締役
の取締役会議長選任はコーポレートガバナンス上、どのような意味を持つのか、最後に私見を述
べたい。 
とりわけ独立社外取締役が議長を務める場合には、独立した立場から取締役会の議論を促し、
監督機能を含む取締役会の実効性向上につながることが期待される。そのためには、経営陣との
適切な連絡・調整や十分な情報提供が重要となる。今回のコーポレートガバナンス・コード改訂
でも、議長や独立社外取締役を支える取締役会事務局（コーポレートセクレタリー等）の機能強

### Page 4

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
4/4 
化の重要性が示されている。社外取締役を議長に選任する企業には、選任によってどのような効
果を目指すのかを明確にし、それに即した支援体制を整えることが重要であろう。 
以 上

【注釈】 
1) 2025 年8 月中旬のプライム市場上場会社（1,618 社）のうち、社外取締役を取締役会議長に選
任している会社数の異同については次のとおり。2026 年 9 月末までの間にプライム市場から退
出した会社（95 社）で社外取締役を取締役会議長に選任していた7 社と、継続上場会社（1,523
社）で社外取締役以外を取締役会議長にした会社1 社の計8 社が減少。継続上場会社のうち17 社
が新規に社外取締役を取締役会議長に選任したので、差し引き9 社の増加となった。新規にプライ
ム市場に上場した会社（20 社）には、社外取締役を取締役会議長に選任していた会社はなかった。 
2) なお、2026 年 9 月末でプライム市場上場会社の全社外取締役数は、6,986 名であり、そのうち
6,793 名（97.2％）が独立社外取締役である。ほとんどの社外取締役は独立社外取締役である
といってよい状況となっている。 
3) 一方、取締役の過半数を独立社外取締役とする会社が着実に増加していることは、河谷
（2026）で示したとおりである。

Document 453: 今年第三次！对冲基金重现“疫情以来罕见同步暴跌”，量化基金遭遇8月以来最惨一日

Source PDF: all report pdf/今年第三次！对冲基金重现“疫情以来罕见同步暴跌”，量化基金遭遇8月以来最惨一日.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

AI 信贷担忧引爆去杠杆浪潮，对冲基⾦⼗个⽉内三度遭遇 " 五年⼀遇 " 极端事件。量化基⾦单
⽇暴跌 -3.7 个标准差，亚洲策略⽉度回撤扩⾄ -6.8% ，韩国 AI 仓位半数已被清洗，⽽中国台
湾仓位仍⾼悬五年 99 百分位。⾼盛警告：整体杠杆率 310% 叠加期权到期缓冲消退，下⼀轮
冲击或从基⾦蔓延⾄⼤盘。
摘要：
AI 信贷担忧引发的去杠杆浪潮，正在以前所未有的频率反复冲击对冲基⾦⾏业。
本周四，对冲基⾦再度经历 " 疫情以来罕⻅的多策略同步⼤跌 "—— 这是 2026 年以来的第三次。⾼
盛 Prime 经纪业务数据显⽰，量化基⾦当⽇遭受 -3.7 个标准差的重创，创下 8 ⽉ 19 ⽇以来最惨单
⽇跌幅。亚洲区对冲基⾦⽉度跌幅扩⼤⾄ -6.8% ，韩国、中国台湾 AI 相关仓位成为重灾区。⾼盛
数据显⽰，基本⾯多空策略当⽇收跌 0.5% ，⽽系统性多空基⾦则⼤幅落后，未能在尾盘随股市
反弹⽽收复失地。
⾼盛 Prime 的 Marco Laicini 在盘中更新中直接点明了这⼀格局的结构性含义：⼗个⽉内发⽣三

次 " 五年⼀遇 " 事件，意味着对冲基⾦⾏业现⾏的 VaR 模型对极端⻛险的评估存在系统性低估，相
关⻛险管理框架或需从根本上重构。市场层⾯，⾼盛 Prime 账簿整体杠杆率仍⾼达 310% ，中国
台湾仓位接近五年⾼点，叠加周五期权到期⽇临近，部分正 gamma 对冲缓冲将随之消退，下⼀
轮去杠杆压⼒或将从对冲基⾦内部蔓延⾄⼤盘指数。
动量因⼦崩塌，做空轧空同步发⽣
此轮抛售的导⽕索在因⼦层⾯⼀⽬了然。
截⾄周四上午 10 时 30 分，⾼盛追踪数据显⽰，动量因⼦（ Momentum ）下跌 3.4 个标准差，拥挤
多头（ Crowded Longs ）下跌 1.8 个标准差，与此同时拥挤空头（ Crowded Shorts ）被轧空上涨
1.2 个标准差。Marco Laicini 将这⼀组合解读为 " 更⼴泛去杠杆 " 的典型信号。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

当多头持仓同步下跌、空头持仓遭到轧空，基⾦经理实际上并⾮在做⽅向性判断，⽽是在同步
削减多空两侧敞⼝。这⼀⾏为所 " 消耗 " 的，正是组合中最为拥挤的因⼦ —— 本轮即为动量因⼦。
具体损伤量化来看，⾼盛 HF VIP 多空配对（最受对冲基⾦⻘睐的多头标的对⽐被做空标的）当
⽇下跌 115 个基点，⾃ 10 ⽉ 8 ⽇本轮调整启动以来累计跌幅已达 -10% 。
量化基⾦从领跑到领跌，仅⽤⼀天
本轮⾏情对量化基⾦的打击尤为讽刺。就在周三，量化系统性基⾦还是⾼盛 Prime 账簿中唯⼀录
得正收益的策略，单⽇上涨 0.5% ，年初⾄今回报⾼达 21.7% ，领跑所有策略。
⼀天之后，全部收益回吐殆尽。收盘时，系统性多空基⾦单⽇跌幅达 -3.7 个标准差，⾼盛图表显
⽰，过去⼀年内仅有 8 ⽉ 19 ⽇⼀个交易⽇跌幅更甚。
⾼盛顶级科技分析师 Peter Callahan 在收盘后的点评中指出，本轮与夏季⾏情有本质区别： 7 ⽉
是仓位层⾯的清洗，⽽此次抛售伴随着实质性的基本⾯疑问 ——AI 资本开⽀的融资速度与可持

续性受到质疑，这对⻛险管理者⽽⾔更难以等待穿越。
多策略基⾦⽅⾯，股票账簿（ 4.5 倍杠杆）当⽇下跌约 1.2% ，与 2 ⽉份暴跌第⼆⽇的跌幅相当。
亚洲成重灾区，韩国仓位半数已被清洗
亚洲市场是此轮抛售最惨烈的战场。
⾼盛 Prime 的 Himanshu Deshmukh 在收盘后报告中披露，亚洲基本⾯多空策略经理周四单⽇亏
损 2.6% ，⽉度回撤扩⼤⾄ -6.8% ，年初⾄今回报降⾄ 16.7% ，回到 "7 ⽉低点⽔平 "—— 距离 6 ⽉底
40% 的⾼点已下跌约 23 个百分点，今年的亮眼成绩被腰斩逾半。
抛售集中在预期⽅向：亚洲股票净卖出 -1.0 个标准差，以韩国和中国台湾为主，多头平仓与空头
回补之⽐⾼达 3 ⽐ 1 ，且韩国、中国台湾的卖盘 " 以 AI 相关多头的解仓为主 " 。⾼盛 2026 年各市场
净流向图显⽰，韩国累计净卖出约占亚洲净市值的 13% ，遥遥领先。

### Page 3

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
韩国仓位净配置⽉内已下降 75 个基点⾄ 3.64% ，处于近⼀年的第 32 百分位，表明该仓位已完成
相当程度的出清。
中国台湾敞⼝⾼悬，成为最⼤潜在⻛险点
相⽐之下，中国台湾的隐患更为突出。⾼盛分析认为，中国台湾仓位在本轮调整中所受冲击 " 相
对有限 " ，原因在于 10 ⽉第⼀周仍有净买⼊⾏为，⽬前总仓位接近历史⾼位，处于近⼀年的第 94
百分位、五年的第 99 百分位。
这意味着韩国的 AI 交易已被半数清洗，⽽中国台湾端的仓位仍停留在近纪录⽔平。据报道，外资
本周单⽇撤出中国台湾资⾦规模⾼达约 93 亿美元，创下历史纪录，⼀旦市场情绪未能改善，潜
在的出逃规模不可⼩觑。
从全球视⻆来看，亚洲信息科技仍占⾼盛 Prime 账簿全球总仓位的约 9% 、全球净敞⼝的约
18% ，⼤约是 2025 年初净仓位占⽐的三倍 —— 即便经历了 7 ⽉的调整。

⾼盛交易部⻔⻅ " 局部超卖 " ，但结构性⻛险未消
并⾮所有声⾳都指向危机。
⾼盛伦敦销售团队在题为 "AI 去⻛险 —— 我们看到的情况 " 的跨部⻔邮件中总结了机构内部观点：
信贷正在 " 迫使市场重新评估 AI 资本开⽀周期的融资速度与落地节奏，仓位解仓放⼤了波动。但
随着 AI 敞⼝⼤幅降低、主动净仓位偏低、利率⾛强，交易台认为存在局部超卖现象，尽管融资
约束仍是实质性问题 " 。
然⽽，结构性压⼒尚未消除。
⾼盛 Prime 账簿整体杠杆率仍达 310% ，且净仓位与总仓位之间的背离已达前所未有的程度；中
国台湾仓位处于五年⾼位；周五期权到期将移除部分正 gamma 缓冲，此前正是这⼀缓冲⼒量使
标普 500 指数在本轮调整中保持相对平稳，周四该指数恰好在 50 ⽇均线附近反弹企稳。

事件  1
10/14 美國 9 ⽉ CPI 年增 3.7% ，核⼼ CPI ⽉增 0.3% ⾼於預期
五⼤重點
1   美國勞⼯統計局公布 9 ⽉ CPI ⽉增 0.6% 、年增 3.7% ，⾼於預期 3.6% 與 8 ⽉ 3.4% ；核⼼ CPI ⽉增
0.3% 、年增 2.6% ，均⾼於預期 0.2% 與 2.5% ， 8 ⽉核⼼為 2.4% 。

2   能源指數⽉增 5.2% 、汽油⽉增 9.1% ，是整體 CPI 加速的主因；住房⽉增 0.4% ，核⼼服務價格
仍有黏性，通膨壓⼒不只來⾃油價。

### Page 2

3   公布後 CME FedWatch 的 10 ⽉升息 1 碼機率由 21.6% 升⾄ 43.7% ，台灣時間 10/15 清晨回落⾄
33.2% ； 12 ⽉前累計升息機率約 90.9% （推算）。
4  2 年期美債殖利率盤中升 12.7 個基點⾄ 4.92% ， 5 年期創 2007 年以來新⾼；美元指數收升 0.37%
⾄ 102.554 ，為 2025 年 5 ⽉以來⾼點。
5   電腦及周邊設備價格年增 9.7% ，為⾄少 2006 年以來最⼤增幅， AI 硬體需求開始推升消費端價
格；現貨⿈⾦同⽇跌 1.37% ⾄每盎司 4,103.8 美元。
三⼤結論
1   核⼼ CPI 年增由 2.4% 升⾄ 2.6% ， 10 ⽉暫停升息不再是定局， 12 ⽉升息成為市場基準情境；政
策利率 3.75% ⾄ 4.00% 仍有上調空間，短天期國庫券仍是承接政策不確定性的⾸選。
2   汽油與住房同步上揚，代表能源衝擊正向服務價格擴散；當核⼼通膨與油價同步上⾏、 10 年
與 30 年期殖利率再創⾼時，應先縮短債券存續期並降低⾼估值成⻑股。

3   美元⾛強與⾦價回落反映實質利率上升，⿈⾦短線承壓；⿈⾦仍是財政與地緣⾵險的避險部
位，維持 10% ⾄ 15% 分批配置，實質利率急升期間暫緩加碼。
事件  2
10/14 費城半導體指數重挫 4.3% 、六連跌， CoreWeave 跌 15%
五⼤重點
1   費城半導體指數收跌 4.3% ，連跌六個交易⽇，為 2025 年 5 ⽉以來最⻑連跌；那斯達克指數跌
1.85% 、標普 500 跌 1.10% ，道瓊僅跌 0.09% 。
2  AI 基礎建設股領跌： CoreWeave 跌 15.29% 、戴爾跌 11.39% 、 Ciena 跌 10.28% ；輝達跌
3.09% 、美光約跌 4% ，台積電 ADR 盤中跌逾 3% 。
3   賣壓來⾃ CPI 偏熱推升殖利率，以及市場對 AI 基建估值與龐⼤資本⽀出的疑慮；戴爾另遭巴克
萊由加碼調降⾄中⽴評等。
4  CoreWeave 執⾏⻑稱近期股價下跌屬市場不理性，並表⽰未交貨訂單⾜以⽀應債務義務，對融
資、銷售與訂單有信⼼。

5   截⾄ 10/14 盤中，費半六個交易⽇累計跌約 10% ，逾半成分股跌幅超過 10% ，其中 Arm 重挫近
20% 、應⽤材料跌約 15% 。
三⼤結論

### Page 3

1   本波修正集中在⾼負債的算⼒租賃與低⽑利組裝環節，與握有議價⼒的先進製程、記憶體不
同；投資上維持先進封裝與記憶體為核⼼， ODM 與算⼒⾦融化部位只作衛星。
2   當 GPU 租賃價格、雲端 ROIC 與⾃由現⾦流轉弱，或 AI 基建債信⽤利差擴⼤時，應降低算⼒租
賃、⾼收益債與⾼負債供應商曝險，增持短券與⿈⾦。
3   殖利率上升壓縮估值不等於 AI 需求反轉；若台積電財報、記憶體報價與客戶資本⽀出仍顯⽰訂
單與現⾦流改善，回檔屬估值重置，可分批⽽⾮追價。
事件  3
10/14 舊⾦⼭聯儲戴利、芝加哥聯儲古爾斯⽐：通膨令⼈失望，但升息不急
五⼤重點
1   舊⾦⼭聯儲總裁戴利接受專訪表⽰，進⼀步升息較可能是必要的，但時點仍在斟酌； 10/27 ⾄
28 會議沒有急迫性，寧可審慎決策。
2   戴利指汽油推升整體通膨，核⼼通膨如預期具黏性；不確定衝擊消退後通膨會否回落，勞動市
場穩定讓聯準會能專注物價穩定。
3   芝加哥聯儲總裁古爾斯⽐稱 9 ⽉通膨「令⼈氣餒」，服務價格頑固顯⽰壓⼒不只來⾃關稅與伊朗

衝突，但他希望升息前看到更多數據。
4   古爾斯⽐更擔⼼ AI 資料中⼼投資造成需求⾯過熱，指需求過熱⼀旦累積便持久且難以逆轉，聯
準會必須事先防範。
5   ⾥奇蒙聯儲總裁巴爾⾦同⽇表⽰ CPI ⾼於他希望看到的⽔準，但不預判 10 ⽉會議結果；三⼈皆
為 2027 年票委，今年無投票權。
三⼤結論
1   官員對升息⽅向有共識、對時點有分歧，「 10 ⽉觀望、 12 ⽉升息」仍是主軸；暫停不等於轉向
寬鬆，⾼估值股票不應以降息預期定價。
2   古爾斯⽐把 AI 投資列為過熱來源，代表 AI 資本⽀出愈強，利率維持⾼檔愈久；美股加碼應以未
履約訂單、⾃由現⾦流與資本報酬率驗證。
3   當 10/15 公布的零售銷售與 PPI 續強、或華許本週公開談話偏鷹時， 10 ⽉升息機率可能再升，
應維持短券與現⾦⽐重，延後⻑債加碼。

### Page 4

事件  4
10/14 Anthropic 第三季營收 168 億美元，季增 45% 並轉為經調整獲利
五⼤重點
1   據出席上市前投資⼈⽇⼈⼠轉述， Anthropic 第三季營收 168 億美元，較第⼆季 116 億美元成⻑
45% ；第⼀季為 47 億美元，兩季內營收增約 2.6 倍（推算）。
2   公司向投資⼈表⽰第三季經調整後獲利並產⽣正現⾦流，最⼤成本項⽬為晶⽚與資料中⼼⽀
出。
3   公司正為⾸次公開發⾏爭取投資⼈⽀持，⽬標估值逾 2 兆美元，上市時點預料延⾄ 11 ⽉美國期
中選舉之後。
4   營收消息發布當天，美股 AI 基建股重挫、 CoreWeave 跌 15% ，模型端營收⾼成⻑與算⼒租賃端
股價⾛弱形成對⽐。
5   數據來⾃投資⼈簡報轉述，公司未正式公告財報，營收認列⼝徑、⽑利率與未調整損益均未揭
露。
三⼤結論
1   模型端營收季增 45% 並達經調整獲利，是 AI 終端變現的具體證據，⽀持算⼒投資可轉為服務收
⼊；但⽤量與收⼊須分開驗證，完整損益仍待上市⽂件揭露。

2   晶⽚與資料中⼼是最⼤成本，代表模型收⼊成⻑會延續對 GPU 、 HBM 與先進封裝的採購； AI
供應鏈⾼分仍集中在具議價權的晶圓代⼯、封裝與記憶體。
3   當模型與軟體收⼊持續成⻑、但算⼒租賃價格與雲端 ROIC 下滑時，應偏重終端變現端與上游
瓶頸環節，降低⾼負債算⼒租賃商部位。
事件  5
10/14 美國銀⾏第三季每股盈餘 0.91 美元遜於預期，摩根⼠丹利營收 203 億美元
五⼤重點
1   美國銀⾏第三季淨利 69.3 億美元、每股 0.91 美元，低於預期 1.10 美元；營收年增 3% ⾄ 300 億美
元，也低於預期約 306 億美元。
2   美國銀⾏獲利受 12 億美元監管罰款拖累，剔除後每股 1.08 美元仍略低於預期；淨利息收⼊年增
8% ⾄ 164 億美元，固定收益交易年減 17% 。

### Page 5

3   美國銀⾏股價開盤重挫 8% ⾄ 5 ⽉以來低點；法說會上分析師質疑其先前稱合規整改費⽤已納⼊
經營成本的揭露，罰款使每股盈餘減少 0.17 美元。
4   摩根⼠丹利第三季營收 203.38 億美元、年增 12% ，每股 3.03 美元；股票業務收⼊年增 41% ⾄
57.99 億美元，財富管理營收 90.65 億美元創新⾼。
5   摩根⼠丹利固定收益收⼊年減 7% ⾄ 20.11 億美元，主因信⽤與商品業務轉弱；財富管理淨新增
資產 2,324 億美元，逾半與 IPO 相關資⾦有關。
三⼤結論
1   美國銀⾏失分主因是⼀次性罰款與交易收⼊下滑，淨利息收⼊仍增 8% ；⾦融股基本⾯未轉
弱，仍可分散半導體集中度，優先資本充⾜的⼤型⾦控。
2   股票交易與財富管理創⾼，反映資本市場與 IPO 熱度延續，承銷與交易是券商獲利⽀撐；但 10
年期殖利率⾼於 5% 時，須留意債券部位評價損失。
3   當信⽤利差擴⼤或⻑端殖利率失序上⾏時，應降低交易收⼊占⽐⾼的券商，保留資產負債表

強、淨利息收⼊穩定的⼤型銀⾏。
事件  6
10/14 艾司摩爾第三季營收 116.6 億歐元，稱 2027 ⾄ 2028 年設備供給無法滿⾜需
求
五⼤重點
1   艾司摩爾第三季營收 116.62 億歐元、⽑利率 57.5% ，淨利 49.6 億歐元、每股 12.95 歐元，優於市
場預期；第四季營收指引 142 億⾄ 152 億歐元、⽑利率約 57% 。
2   公司預估 2026 全年營收 440 億⾄ 450 億歐元、⽑利率約 56% ；執⾏⻑表⽰客戶持續上調短中期
設備需求預測，部分客戶已洽詢 2029 甚⾄ 2030 年訂單。
3   執⾏⻑表⽰依⽬前規劃， 2027 與 2028 年供給都無法完全滿⾜客戶需求，正研究在先前宣布的
2027 年產能增加 30% 之外，再擴充 2028 年產能。
4   艾司摩爾歐股收漲 1.2% ，帶動斯托克 600 指數漲 0.5% ；同晚美股時段其 ADR 隨費半⾛弱，盤
中跌 1.44% 。
5   執⾏⻑指出記憶體在設備需求中的重要性持續提⾼，與 HBM 、 DDR5 擴產帶動先進設備需求的

⽅向⼀致。

### Page 6

三⼤結論
1   設備供給成為 2027 ⾄ 2028 年瓶頸，晶圓與記憶體擴產節奏受上游限制，先進製程與封裝的稀
缺性與議價⼒可延續；台積電與記憶體⿓頭仍是 AI 供應鏈核⼼部位。
2   歐股配置以低負債、跨國現⾦流企業為主，艾司摩爾符合此條件；但歐股整體仍受法國財政與
能源成本牽制，應以個股選擇⽽⾮指數全⾯加碼。
3   當客戶設備需求預測續升、第四季營收落在指引區間時，應維持半導體設備部位；若客戶下修
短中期需求預測，應下修設備類股評價。
事件  7
10/14 中國 9 ⽉出⼝年增 36.3% ，創 2 ⽉以來最快
五⼤重點
1   海關總署公布 9 ⽉出⼝以美元計年增 36.3% ⾄ 4,471.8 億美元，⾼於 8 ⽉ 25% 與預期約 25% ，為 2
⽉以來最快；進⼝年增 31% ⾄ 3,136.5 億美元。
2   以⼈⺠幣計， 9 ⽉進出⼝ 5.16 兆元、⾸度突破 5 兆元，年增 27.5% ； 1 ⾄ 9 ⽉出⼝以美元計年增
21.3% ⾄ 33,707 億美元。

3   海關總署表⽰前三季積體電路出⼝成⻑ 99.1% ，⼯業機器⼈與電⼦元件等產品進出⼝合計逾 10
兆元、成⻑ 58.9% ， AI 與電⼒設備是主要動能。
4   以⼈⺠幣計前三季進⼝成⻑ 22.3% 、快於出⼝ 16.4% ，其中儲存部件進⼝成⻑ 1.5 倍； 9 ⽉貿易
順差約 1,335 億美元（推算）。
5   海關總署稱對美國貿易持續回升，並認為第四季出⼝基本⾯沒有改變，外貿進出⼝持續成⻑有
⽀撐。
三⼤結論
1   出⼝⾼增集中於積體電路與電⼦元件，與台韓 AI 出⼝相互印證，代表 AI 需求具區域廣度；但中
國零售與投資仍弱，出⼝強勁不應解讀為內需復甦。
2   中美貿易休戰僅延⻑⾄ 2027 年 1 ⽉ 10 ⽇，稀⼟、農產品與 H200 交貨仍有履約⾵險；中國股市
維持低權重，只選擇性配置⾼技術製造。
3   當中國零售、⺠間投資與房地產資⾦同步改善時，才應提⾼中國資產⽐重；在此之前，出⼝動
能較適合透過上游電⼦供應鏈表達。

### Page 7

事件  8
10/14 國際能源署⽉報：柴油吃緊延續⾄ 2027 年，下修 2027 年供給回升幅度
五⼤重點
1   國際能源署 10 ⽉⽯油市場報告指出， 9 ⽉全球⽯油供給⽉增每⽇ 250 萬桶⾄ 1.023 億桶，
OPEC+ 增產每⽇ 210 萬桶、以沙烏地為主；但波斯灣產油國（不含阿曼）出⼝仍較戰前低 33% 。
2   國際能源署將 2027 年全球供給回升幅度下修⾄每⽇ 770 萬桶（前估 800 萬桶），預估 2026 年
OPEC 產量減少每⽇ 650 萬桶、 2027 年回增 500 萬桶。
3   第三季柴油供給減少約每⽇ 170 萬桶，原因是伊朗衝突阻斷霍爾⽊茲運輸、烏克蘭攻擊迫使俄
羅斯煉油廠減產；柴油吃緊將延續⾄ 2027 年，庫存回補最早 2027 年下半年。
4   報告發布後布蘭特 12 ⽉期貨收漲 0.35% ⾄每桶 106.06 美元， WTI 11 ⽉期貨收漲 1.01% ⾄ 95.09
美元，布蘭特仍站穩 100 美元之上。
5   新加坡海事及港務管理局 10/14 晚間聲明，兩艘新加坡籍貨櫃船在霍爾⽊茲海峽遭不明投射物

擊中，其中⼀艘起⽕沉沒，船員均安全撤離。
三⼤結論
1   柴油吃緊延續⾄ 2027 年，能源成本對運輸與⾷品價格的傳導尚未結束；當油價再上⾏並帶動
核⼼服務通膨時，聯準會暫停升息的定價可能迅速逆轉。
2   原油維持事件避險定位：當霍爾⽊茲航運再受攻擊、布蘭特續站 100 美元以上時，可⽤低成
本、⾼股息能源股或⼩部位期貨對沖；航運與庫存回補確認後應降低部位。
3  2027 年 OPEC 預估回增每⽇ 500 萬桶，⾼基期與供給恢復有助通膨回落，這是原油⻑線評分低
於現況的原因；不宜把事件性⾼油價當成⻑期核⼼資產。
事件  9
10/14 中國 9 ⽉ PPI 年增 5.0% ， CPI 年增 0.9% 低於預期
五⼤重點
1   國家統計局公布 9 ⽉ PPI 年增 5.0% 、⽉增 1.2% ，年增幅較 8 ⽉擴⼤ 1.2 個百分點；⼯業⽣產者購
進價格年增 6.9% ， 1 ⾄ 9 ⽉ PPI 平均年增 2.3% 。
2  9 ⽉ CPI 年增 0.9% ，低於預期 1.0% ，前值 0.8% ； CPI ⽉增 0.2% ，漲幅較 8 ⽉回落 0.2 個百分點。

### Page 8

3  CPI 結構中⾷品價格年減 1.7% 、⾮⾷品上漲 1.5% ，服務價格僅漲 0.8% ；國家統計局指能源價
格上漲是 CPI 年增幅擴⼤的主要推⼿。
4  PPI 與 CPI 年增率差距擴⼤⾄ 4.1 個百分點（推算），上游價格漲勢未傳導⾄消費端；購進價格漲
幅⾼於出廠價格，中下游企業成本上升快於售價（推算）。
5  1 ⾄ 9 ⽉ CPI 平均年增 0.9% ，城市與農村 9 ⽉ CPI 分別上漲 0.9% 與 0.8% ，城鄉物價皆溫和，未⾒
需求⾯全⾯回溫。
三⼤結論
1  PPI 上揚主要反映油價與原料成本⽽⾮終端需求擴張，中國產出回升仍集中於能源煉化與⾼技
術，尚未擴散⾄居⺠消費；通縮壓⼒緩解不等於內需復甦。
2  CPI 僅 0.9% ，貨幣政策仍有寬鬆空間，降準是較可⾏⼯具；但信⽤投放若未進⼊居⺠收⼊與房
地產需求，寬鬆效果仍偏供給側。
3   當 PPI 續升⽽ CPI 持續低於 1% 時，應避開缺乏轉嫁能⼒的中游製造；中國股市維持選擇性配
置，等待零售與⺠間投資改善再提⾼權重。

事件  10
10/14 中國⼈⾏宣布 7,000 億元 6 個⽉期買斷式逆回購， 9 ⽉ M2 年增 7.2%
五⼤重點
1   中國⼈⾏宣布 10/15 以固定數量、利率招標⽅式開展 7,000 億元買斷式逆回購，期限 6 個⽉（ 182
天），到期⽇ 2027 年 4 ⽉ 15 ⽇，⽬的是保持銀⾏體系流動性充裕。
2  9 ⽉末 M2 （廣義貨幣）餘額 359.63 兆元、年增 7.2% ；社會融資規模存量年增 7.1% ，前三季社
融增量 27.46 兆元，較去年同期少 2.63 兆元。
3   前三季⼈⺠幣貸款增加 11.52 兆元、存款增加 20.76 兆元； 9 ⽉銀⾏間同業拆借⽉加權平均利率
為 1.40% 。
4   中國⼈⾏表⽰，前三季債券與股票融資在社融增量中的占⽐超過貸款約 7 個百分點，直接融資
⽐重上升，貸款需求相對疲弱。
5   同⽇ 7 天期逆回購操作利率維持 1.40% ；近 72 ⼩時中國⼈⾏未調整政策利率或存款準備⾦率。
三⼤結論

### Page 9

1   買斷式逆回購是補充中期流動性⽽⾮降息，政策利率維持 1.40% ；社融增量少於去年，信⽤需
求仍弱，寬鬆仍偏數量⼯具，降準是較可⾏的下⼀步。
2   存款增幅遠⼤於貸款，資⾦停留在銀⾏體系⽽未進⼊消費與房地產，這是中國內需與股市評分
偏低的原因；中國股市維持低權重、選擇性配置。
3   當中國⼈⾏降準或降息，且貸款與房地產到位資⾦同步回升時，才應提⾼中國資產權重；若只
有數量投放⽽零售仍弱，應維持低配。
待確認
1. 推算值：事件 1 「 12 ⽉前累計升息機率約 90.9% 」以 CME FedWatch 12 ⽉升 1 碼 62.2% 加升 2 碼 28.7% 推算；事件 4
「兩季營收增約 2.6 倍」以 168 億與 47 億美元推算；事件 7 貿易順差 1,335 億美元以出⼝減進⼝推算；事件 9 PPI 與 CPI
差距 4.1 個百分點及購進、出廠價差為推算。
2. 單⼀來源：事件 4 Anthropic 營收、經調整獲利、正現⾦流與估值⽬標均為出席投資⼈⽇⼈⼠轉述，公司未公告，

第⼆季營收另有 115 億美元的說法；事件 1 電腦價格年增 9.7% 為媒體整理之細項。
3. 事件 2 ：費半收盤點位未取得，六連跌累計約 10% 為 10/14 盤中統計，收盤後累計跌幅應更⼤；個股跌幅為收盤快
訊。事件 3 ：戴利為專訪轉述、古爾斯⽐為電視專訪，均⾮官⽅講稿。事件 7 ： 9 ⽉出⼝預期值有 25% 與 25.5% 兩種說
法。
4. 事件 5 ：美國銀⾏數據取⾃財報新聞整理，官⽅新聞稿未逐項核對；股價開盤跌 8% ，收盤跌幅各來源介於 5% ⾄ 6%
以上。摩根⼠丹利數據已核對其 SEC 8-K 。
5. 與上⼀輪⽐對（上⼀輪資料同步 10/14 07:50 ）：排除重複主題 —10/13 紐約聯儲威廉斯與波⼠頓聯儲柯林斯談話、
10/13 摩根⼤通與花旗財報、 10/13 胡塞攻擊沙烏地阿美、 10/13 KOSPI 暴跌、 10/13 新台幣貶值與外資賣超、應⽤材
料 2030 年⽬標、 IMF 世界經濟展望、 OPEC ⽉報、⽇本 9 ⽉企業物價及同⽇⽇銀植⽥國會答詢與家庭通膨預期調查、

華為麒麟晶⽚。
6. 依新⼀⼿事實另⽴事件：事件 2 費半 10/14 收盤六連跌（台灣時間 10/15 04:01 ）、事件 5 美國銀⾏與摩根⼠丹利財報

輪資料同步時間 10/14 07:50 。
7. 利率與準備⾦： 72 ⼩時內無主要央⾏實際調整利率或準備⾦率；中國⼈⾏ 7 天期逆回購利率維持 1.40% ，新加坡⾦
管局 10/14 意外維持政策不變，均未調整利率。
8. 九⼤分類⽅⾯，台灣經濟與央⾏：近 72 ⼩時無重⼤官⽅數據或政策；台灣公司：台積電第三季財報於台灣時間

### Page 1

十月 06, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  1

動態更新  工業自動化‧台灣

上銀 
(2049.TW /2049 TT)

上升週期延續，獲利調升可期   
重要訊息   
上銀 3Q26 營收年增率加速至 35%，分別優於市場共識及我們預估 3%與
6%。儘管人力短缺可能限制短期營收動能，我們認為客戶資本支出持續上
修，加上出口成長加速帶動製造業活動升溫，將支撐本波上升週期延續至
2027 年。 
評論及分析  
3Q26 營收表現超前。9 月營收達 27.2 億元，年增 30.1%、月增 0.4%，帶
動 3Q26 營收年增 35%、季增 8%。受惠 AI 與半導體相關應用訂單強勁，
以及產品漲價，3Q26 營收表現優於季節性，並分別較市場共識與我們預估
高出 3%與 6%。管理層表示亞洲及美國市場需求維持強勁，歐洲訂單亦開
始出現復甦跡象。考量產能持續滿載及產品組合 有利，我們預估毛利率將季
增 0.8 個百分點至 35.0%。加上營運槓桿效益，我們預期營益率進一步走升
將帶動每股盈餘達 2.89 元，較凱基原估與市場共識高出 8%、7%。 
自動化上升週期可望延續至 2027 年。上銀指出目前主要瓶頸仍為人力短
缺，因接單持續超過產能供給，滾珠螺桿與線性滑軌交期分別維持於六個月
及五個月的高檔水準。雖然公司已積極增聘移工並提高加班時數，但我們認
為相關措施轉化為明顯營收貢獻仍需時間。受惠有利的產業動態，我們目前
預估 4Q26 營收將穩健年增 21%，季減幅度收斂至 3%。展望後市，我們預
期客戶資本支出持續上修，加上出口成長加速及 PMI 指標回升所反映的製造
業活動增溫，可望支撐本波自動化上升週期延續至 2027 年。 
獲利進一步上修可望推升評價。 考量 3Q26 營收優於預期，以及  AI 驅動的
半導體需求持續擴大至更多應用領域，我們預期市場將在 11 月 10 日財報公
布前進一步調升上銀獲利預估。屆時，我們預期管理層將釋出樂觀的 2027
年展望，反映客戶持續擴大資本支出與產能，進一步強化公司成長動能。我
們分別調升 2026- 27 年獲利預估 4%/1%，且隨著投資人對本波上升週期延
續性的信心增強，我們預期市場共識獲利預估仍有進一步上修空間，進而帶
動評價提升。  
投資建議  
我們持續看好上銀，預期自動化上升週期延續加上共識獲利上修將推升股價
上檔。目標價調整為 482 元，根據 35 倍本益比。重申「增加持股」評等。  
投資風險  
下檔風險包括資本支出週期延宕、新台幣升值。

圖 7: 公司概況  圖 8: 亞洲為上銀主要銷售市場 
上銀為全球第二大線性 滑軌產品製造商 ，僅次於 THK (6481 
JP)，公司研發並製造線性移動零組件， 包括線性滑軌與滾珠螺
桿。線性滑軌為公司收入大宗，佔整體營收的 61%，其次為
滾珠螺桿 (20%)、工業機器人  (9%) 與其他 (10%)。目前亞洲 
(不含台灣) 為其最大市場，營收佔比達 63%，其次為歐洲  
(26%)、美洲 (6%) 與台灣 (5%)。
